Introduction into Noncommutative Algebra
Aleks Kleyn

Abstract
. I dedicated the volume 2 2 of monograph ’Introduction into Noncommutative Algebra’ to studying of module over non-commutative algebra.

Chapter 1
Preface

1.1.
Why Did I Remember Doctor Ouch

And the mountains stand in front of him on his way,

And he begins to creep over the mountains,

And the mountains get higher,

And the mountains get steeper,

And the mountains go right up to the clouds!

Kornei Chukovsky. Doctor Ouch.

When I was a child I was interested in the question of why it was so difficult
for doctor Ouch to get to Africa.

1.1
1.1

1.1

Alicia Baca
translated fairy poem ”Doctor Ouch” by Kornei Chukovsky
into English.
You can see this translation on web site

http://zvezda-zornitsa.livejournal.com/

(©2013 zvezda_zornitsa).
Alicia Baca permitted me to use fragment of
her translation in the epigraph to this book.

Jackal from Africa rode up to doctor Ouch on the mare.
Tanya and Vanya just ran to Africa.
But doctor Ouch had to swim across the sea, climb mountains.

I found the answer later, when I read this fairy poem to my children.
There are doctors who risk their lives to go
where the epidemic is raging. Their goal is to save people.

However, in this book, which is far from medicine and feats,
I recalled a fairy poem by Chukovsky for another reason.

I was writing this book for a long time, with long intervals.
Sometimes I had realized that I was not ready to prove a particular statement.
Sometimes a chapter flies away from the book and becomes a basis
for other book or paper.
I am on the home stretch now.
But wait, am I indeed?
At least, I know what I will write and
I see contours of statements that will remain outside the book.

When I tryed to understand what I am feeling,
I could not help remembering these lines.

And the mountains stand in front of him on his way

Kornei Chukovsky. Doctor Ouch.

In his genial fairy poem, Chukovsky wrote not only about doctors.
A team of alpinists decided to storm the mountain
which had not previously been climbed.
They do not have map of this mountain. They see
summit above, and this summit is their goal.
However when they are close to the summit, it becomes clear that this is
a mountain pass or a small plateau, and summit is ahead.

In science research, often the solution of a problem
opens the way to solve other
more advance problems.

1.2.
Evolution of Point of View

In the book
[
5
],
I considered one of the central question of my following research.
It was the question about structure of vector space
V
over division algebra
A
.
In particular, it follows from this paper
that we can represent linear transformation of
A
-vector space
V

as matrix of
A\otimes A
-numbers.
This statement became the basis of my research in calculus over
non-commutative algebra. And the following research confirmed
that this statement is true.

However I supposed also that coordinates of a vector
of
A
-vector space
V
are
A\otimes A
-numbers also.
At the level of intuition this statement appears to be true.
A modul of dimension
1
was one of the riddles
that I had to solve.
Identification of this module and tensor
square of algebra contradicted to the tradition of identifying
a module of dimension
1
and ring over which this module is generated.
That is why I did not included
the statement about coordinates of vector into the book
[
5
]
and over the following years I tried to answer this question.

It took ten years of intense searching
to find the answer to my question that concerns me.
During this time, my point of view on the module
over non-commutative algebra has changed a bit.
The impetus for this change was the theorem
[
8
]-

and the corollary
[
8
]-
.
It was first case when I considered the diagram of representations.

We can study a module over non-commutative ring.
However, considering that non-commutative ring is algebra
over the center of ring, then study of module over corresponding algebra
gives more wide picture.
Therefore I decided to separate modules over non-commutative ring
as separate topic and to consider module over non-commutative
algebra. However, algebra may be non-associative.
I also decided to split description of left and right modules.
I will explain the reason below.

Linear map of algebra over commutative ring has following form

(1.2.1)

(a\otimes b)\circ x=axb

It is natural to expect that linear map of module
over
D
-algebra has the same format. However, this is where the root of the problem was.
At first glance, tensor

a\otimes b

acts on
A
-number
x
from left and from right.
However, if we take into account the law of composition

(c\otimes d)\circ(a\otimes b)=ca\otimes bd

then it becomes clear that the equality
(
1.2.1
)
generates left representation of algebra
A\otimes A
in algebra
A
.
Therefore, if we assume that coordinates of vector in module over
D
-algebra
have the form

a\otimes b

,
then it becomes clear that it is not enough one tensor for description
of linear transformation of module, but two tensors are needed.

Like ten years ago,
calculus was the tool
which helped me to find the answer.
Since derivative is example of linear map,
I decided to consider differentiation of the map of multiple variables.

Consider the map

f:V\rightarrow W

defined by the equality

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=p(x,y,z)=x^{2}y^{3}+xz^{2}x

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=q(x,y,z)=xzyx+x^{2}yxy

Partial derivatives of the map
f
have form

\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}=(1\otimes xy^{3})+(x\otimes y^{3})+(1\otimes z^{2}x)+(xz^{2}\otimes 1)

\frac{\partial v}{\partial y}=(x^{2}\otimes y^{2})+(x^{2}y\otimes y)+(x^{2}y^{2}\otimes 1)

\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}=(x\otimes zx)+(xz\otimes x)

and differential of the map
f
has form

\displaystyle dv

\displaystyle=dx\,xy^{3}+x\,dx\,y^{3}+dx\,z^{2}x+xz^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle+x^{2}\,dy\,y^{2}+x^{2}y\,dy\,y+x^{2}y^{2}\,dy

\displaystyle+x\,dz\,zx+xz\,dz\,x

Therefore, displacement vectors have form

\begin{matrix}\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}dx\\
dy\\
dz\end{pmatrix}&\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}dv\\
dw\end{pmatrix}\end{matrix}

and we can write the derivative of the map
f
as

\begin{pmatrix}dv\\
dw\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}&\displaystyle\frac{\partial v}{\partial y}&\displaystyle\frac{\partial v}{\partial z}\\[10.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}&\displaystyle\frac{\partial w}{\partial y}&\displaystyle\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\end{pmatrix}{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}\begin{pmatrix}dx\\
dy\\
dz\end{pmatrix}

Therefore, the module
V
is not
A\otimes A
-module,
but
A
-module. But is it a left module or a right module?
And why was this format of product of matrices chosen?

Chapter 2
Representation of Universal Algebra

This chapter
contains definitions and theorems
which are necessary for an understanding of the text of this
book.
So the reader may read the statements from this
chapter
in process of reading the main text of the
book.

Convention 2.0.1
.

Element of
\Omega
-algebra
A
is called

A
-number
.
For instance, complex number is also called

C
-number, and quaternion is called
H
-number.

∎

Representation of Universal Algebra

.

Let the set
A_{2}
be
\Omega_{2}
-algebra.
Let

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A_{1}

in

-algebra
A_{2}

For

,

The tuple of maps

Remark 2.1.5
.

We may consider a pair of maps
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
as map

F:A_{1}\cup A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}\cup B_{2}

such that

F(A_{1})=B_{1}\ \ \ \ F(A_{2})=B_{2}

Therefore, hereinafter the tuple of maps

r=(r_{1},r_{2})

also is called map
and we will use map

r:f\rightarrow g

Let

a=(a_{1},a_{2})

be tuple of
A
-numbers.
We will use notation

r(a)=(r_{1}(a_{1}),r_{2}(a_{2}))

for image of tuple of
A
-numbers
with respect to morphism of representations
r
.

∎

Remark 2.1.6
.

Consider morphism of representations

(r_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1},\ r_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2})

We denote elements of the set
B_{1}
by letter using pattern
b\in B_{1}
.
However if we want to show that
b
is image of element

a\in A_{1}
,

we use notation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}
.
Thus equation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}=r_{1}(a)

means that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a)}
(in left part of equation)
is image

a\in A_{1}

(in right part of equation).
Using such considerations, we denote
element of set
B_{2}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.8}r_{2}(m)}
.
We will follow this convention when we consider correspondences
between homomorphisms of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
and maps between sets
where we defined corresponding representations.

∎

‘

2.2.
\Omega
-Group

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let sum be defined
in
\Omega
-algebra
A
.
A map

f:A\rightarrow A

of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A

is called

additive map

if

f(a+b)=f(a)+f(b)

∎

Definition 2.2.2
.

A map

g:A^{n}\rightarrow A

is called

polyadditive map

if for any

i
,
i=1
, …,
n
,

f(a_{1},...,a_{i}+b_{i},...,a_{n})=f(a_{1},...,a_{i},...,a_{n})+f(a_{1},...,b_{i},...,a_{n})

∎

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let sum which is not necessarily commutative
be defined
in
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
.
We use the symbol
+
to denote sum.
Let

\Omega=\Omega_{1}\setminus\{+\}

If
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is group
relative to sum
and any operation
\omega\in\Omega

is polyadditive map,
then
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is called

\Omega
-group
.
If
\Omega
-group
A
is associative group
relative to sum,
then
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is called

associative
\Omega
-group
.
If
\Omega
-group
A
is Abelian group
relative to sum,
then
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is called

Abelian
\Omega
-group
.

∎

Theorem 2.2.4
.

Let

\omega\in\Omega(n)

be polyadditive map.
The operation
\omega

is distributive

over addition

a_{1}...(a_{i}+b_{i})...a_{n}\omega=a_{1}...a_{i}...a_{n}\omega+a_{1}...b_{i}...a_{n}\omega\ \ \ i=1,...,n

Proof.
The theorem follows from definitions

2.2.2
,

2.2.3
.
∎

Definition 2.2.5
.

Let product

c_{1}=a_{1}*b_{1}

be operation of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
.
Let

\Omega=\Omega_{1}\setminus\{*\}
.

If
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is group
with respect to product
and, for any operation

\omega\in\Omega(n)
,

the product is distributive over the operation
\omega

a*(b_{1}...b_{n}\omega)=(a*b_{1})...(a*b_{n})\omega

(b_{1}...b_{n}\omega)*a=(b_{1}*a)...(b_{n}*a)\omega

then
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is called

multiplicative
\Omega
-group
.

∎

Definition 2.2.6
.

If

(2.2.1)

a*b=b*a

then multiplicative
\Omega
-group is called

Abelian
.

∎

Definition 2.2.7
.

Let sum

c_{1}=a_{1}+b_{1}

which is not necessarily commutative
and product

c_{1}=a_{1}*b_{1}

be operations of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
.
Let

\Omega=\Omega_{1}\setminus\{+,*\}
.

If
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is

\Omega\cup\{*\}
-group
and
multiplicative
\Omega\cup\{+\}
-group,
then
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A
is called

\Omega
-ring
.

∎

Theorem 2.2.8
.

The product in
\Omega
-ring

is distributive

over addition

\displaystyle a*(b_{1}+b_{2})

\displaystyle=a*b_{1}+a*b_{2}

\displaystyle(b_{1}+b_{2})*a

\displaystyle=b_{1}*a+b_{2}*a

Proof.
The theorem follows from the definitions

2.2.3
,

2.2.5
,

2.2.7
.

∎

Definition 2.2.9
.

Let
A
be
\Omega
-ring. The

matrix

over
\Omega
-ring
A
is a table of
A
-numbers
a^{i}_{j}

where the index
i
is the number of row
and the index
j
is the number of column.

∎

Convention 2.2.10
.

We will use Einstein summation convention.
When an index is present in
an expression twice (one above and one below) and a set of index is known,
we have the sum with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

a^{i}v_{i}=\sum_{i\in I}a^{i}v_{i}

If needed to clearly
show set of index, I will do it.

∎

According to the custom the product of
matrices
a
and
b
is defined as product of
rows of the matrix
a
and columns of the matrix
b
.

If the product in

\Omega
-ring
is non-commutative,
we cannot confine ourselves to traditional product of matrices
and we need to define two products of matrices.
To distinguish between these products we introduced
a new notation.

Definition 2.2.11
.

Let the nubmer of columns of the matrix
a
equal the number of rows of the matrix
b
.

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product

of matrices
a
and
b
has form

(2.2.2)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b}=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}\end{pmatrix}

(2.2.3)

{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}

(2.2.4)

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}\end{pmatrix}{}_{*}{}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it p}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}&=\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\
...&...&...\\
{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a{}_{*}{}^{*}b})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product can be expressed as product of a row of the matrix
a

over a column of the matrix
b
.

∎

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\\

{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product can be expressed as product of a column of the matrix

over a row of the matrix

.

∎

We also consider following operations on the set of matrices.

Definition 2.2.13
.

The transpose
a^{T}
of the matrix
a

exchanges rows and columns

(2.2.8)

{(a^{T})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

∎

Theorem 2.2.14
.

Double transpose is original matrix

(2.2.9)

(a^{T})^{T}=a

Proof.
The theorem follows from the definition

2.2.13
.

∎

Definition 2.2.15
.

The sum of matrices
a
and
b
is defined by the equality

(2.2.10)

{(a+b)}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}+{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}

∎

Remark 2.2.16
.

We will use symbol
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-   or
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-   in name of properties of each product
and in the notation.
We can read the symbol

{}_{*}{}^{*}
as

rc
-product (product of row over column)
and the symbol
{}^{*}{}_{*}
as

cr
-product (product of column over row).
In order to keep this
notation consistent with the existing one we assume that
we have in mind
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product when
no clear notation is present.

This rule we extend to following terminology.
To draw symbol of product,
we put two symbols of product in the place
of index which participate in sum.
For instance, if product of
A
-numbers has form

a\circ b
,
then
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product
of matrices
a
and
b
has form

a{}_{\circ}{}^{\circ}b

and
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product
of matrices
a
and
b
has form

a{}^{\circ}{}_{\circ}b
.

∎

Theorem 2.2.19
.

(2.2.19)

(a{}_{*}{}^{*}b)^{T}=a^{T}{}^{*}{}_{*}b^{T}

Theorem 2.2.20
.

(2.2.20)

(a{}^{*}{}_{*}b)^{T}=(a^{T}){}_{*}{}^{*}(b^{T})

Proof of theorem

2.2.19
.

The chain of equalities

(2.2.21)

{((a{}_{*}{}^{*}b)^{T})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}={(a{}_{*}{}^{*}b)}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}={(a^{T})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}{(b^{T})}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}={((a^{T}){}^{*}{}_{*}(b^{T}))}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

follows from (
2.2.8
),
(
2.2.2
) and
(
2.2.5
).
The equality (
2.2.19
) follows from
(
2.2.21
).

∎

Proof of theorem

2.2.20
.

We can prove (
2.2.20
)
in case of matrices the same way as we
proved (
2.2.19
). However it is
more important for us to show that (
2.2.20
)
follows directly from (
2.2.19
).

Applying (
2.2.9
) to each term in left side of
(
2.2.20
) we get

(2.2.22)

(a{}^{*}{}_{*}b)^{T}=((a^{T})^{T}{}^{*}{}_{*}(b^{T})^{T})^{T}

From (
2.2.22
) and (
2.2.19
)
it follows that

(2.2.23)

(a{}^{*}{}_{*}b)^{T}=((a^{T}{}_{*}{}^{*}b^{T})^{T})^{T}

(
2.2.20
) follows from (
2.2.23
)
and (
2.2.9
).

∎

Definition 2.2.21
.

The set of matrices
\mathcal{A}
is a
biring

if we defined on
\mathcal{A}
an unary operation, say transpose,
and three binary operations,
say
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product,
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product and sum, such that

•

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product and sum define structure of ring on
\mathcal{A}

•

{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product and sum define structure of ring on
\mathcal{A}

•

both products have common identity

E_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}=({\delta}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}})\ \ \ \boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}\leq\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}},\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}\leq\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}

∎

Theorem 2.2.22
.

Transpose of identity is identity

(2.2.24)

E_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}^{T}=E_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from definitions

2.2.13
,

2.2.21
.

∎

Theorem 2.2.23

(duality principle for biring)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be true statement about
biring
A
.
If we exchange the same time

•

a\in A
and
a^{T}

•

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product and
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product

then we soon get true statement.

Theorem 2.2.24

(duality principle for biring of matrices)
.

Let
A
be biring of matrices.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be true statement about matrices.
If we exchange the same time

•

rows and columns of all matrices

•

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product and
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product

then we soon get true statement.

Proof.
This is the immediate consequence
of the theorem
2.2.23
.
∎

Remark 2.2.25
.

If product in
\Omega
-
A
ring is commutative, then

(2.2.25)

a{}_{*}{}^{*}b=({a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}{b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}})=({b}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}{a}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}})=b{}^{*}{}_{*}a

Reducible biring
is
the biring which holds

condition of reducibility of products

(
2.2.25
).
So, in reducible biring, it is enough to consider only

{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product. However in case when
the order of factors is essential
we will use
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product also.
∎

2.3.
Representation of Multiplicative
\Omega
-Group

We assume that transformations of representation

\textstyle{g:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}

may act on
A_{2}
-numbers either on the left or on the right.
We must consider an endomorphism of
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
as operator.
In such case, the following conditions must be satisfied.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

be a multiplicative
\Omega
-group with product
2.3
2.3

2.3

Very often a product
in multiplicative
\Omega
-group

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

is superposition of endomorphisms

f\bullet g=f\circ g

However, a product
in multiplicative
\Omega
-group

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

may be different from superposition of endomorphisms.

(f,g)\rightarrow f\bullet g

Let an endomorphism
f
act on
A_{2}
-number
a
on the left.
We will use notation

(2.3.1)

f(a_{2})=f\bullet a_{2}

Let
A_{1}
be multiplicative
\Omega
-group with product

(a,b)\rightarrow a*b

We call a homomorphism of multiplicative
\Omega
-group

(2.3.2)

f:A_{1}\rightarrow\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

left-side representation

of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

or

left-side
A_{1}
-representation

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
if the map
f
holds

(2.3.3)

f(a_{1}*b_{1})\bullet a_{2}=(f(a_{1})\bullet f(b_{1}))\bullet a_{2}

We identify
an
A_{1}
-number
a_{1}
and its image
f(a_{1})

and write left-side transformation
caused by
A_{1}
-number
a_{1}

as

a_{2}^{\prime}=f(a_{1})\bullet a_{2}=a_{1}*a_{2}

In this case, the equality
(
2.3.3
)
gets following form

(2.3.4)

f(a_{1}*b_{1})\bullet a_{2}=(a_{1}*b_{1})*a_{2}

The map

(a_{1},a_{2})\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\rightarrow a_{1}*a_{2}\in A_{2}

generated by left-side representation
f
is called

left-side product

of
A_{2}
-number
a_{2}
over
A_{1}
-number
a_{1}
.

∎

From the equality
(
2.3.4
),
it follows that

(2.3.5)

(a_{1}*b_{1})*a_{2}=a_{1}*(b_{1}*a_{2})

We consider the equality
(
2.3.5
)
as

associative law
.

Example 2.3.2
.

Let

f:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}

g:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}

be endomorphisms of
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
.
Let product
in multiplicative
\Omega
-group

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

is composition of endomorphisms.
Since the product of maps
f
and
g
is defined in the same order
as these maps act on
A_{2}
-number,
then we consider the equality

(2.3.6)

(f\circ g)\circ a=f\circ(g\circ a)

as
associative law.
This allows writing of equality
(
2.3.6
)
without using of brackets

f\circ g\circ a=f\circ(g\circ a)=(f\circ g)\circ a

as well it allows writing of equality
(
2.3.3
)
in the following form

(2.3.7)

f(a_{1}*b_{1})\circ a_{2}=f(a_{1})\circ f(b_{1})\circ a_{2}

∎

Remark 2.3.3
.

Let the map

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

be the left-side representation
of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
.
Let the map

\textstyle{g:B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{B_{2}}

be the left-side representation
of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
B_{1}

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
B_{2}
.
Let the map

(r_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1},\ r_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2})

be morphism of representations.
We use notation

r_{2}(a_{2})=r_{2}\circ a_{2}

for image of
A_{2}
-number
a_{2}

with respect to the map
r_{2}
.
Then we can write the equality
(
2.1.2
)
in the following form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.8}r_{2}\circ(a_{1}*a_{2})}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r_{1}(a_{1})}*({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.8}r_{2}\circ a_{2}})

∎

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

be a multiplicative
\Omega
-group with product
2.4
2.4

2.4

Very often a product
in multiplicative
\Omega
-group

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

is superposition of endomorphisms

f\bullet g=f\circ g

However, a product
in multiplicative
\Omega
-group

\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

may be different from superposition of endomorphisms.

(f,g)\rightarrow f\bullet g

Let an endomorphism
f
act on
A_{2}
-number
a
on the right.
We will use notation

(2.3.8)

f(a_{2})=a_{2}\bullet f

Let
A_{1}
be multiplicative
\Omega
-group with product

(a,b)\rightarrow a*b

We call a homomorphism of multiplicative
\Omega
-group

(2.3.9)

f:A_{1}\rightarrow\End(\Omega_{2},A_{2})

right-side representation

of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

or

right-side

-representation

in
\Omega_{2}

A_{2}
if the map
f

We identify

We consider the equality

.

Example 2.3.5
.

Let

f:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}

be endomorphisms of

in multiplicative

Remark 2.3.6
.

Let the map

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

∎

If multiplicative
\Omega

then there is no difference between left-side and right-side representations.

Definition 2.3.7
.

Let
A_{1}
be Abelian multiplicative
\Omega
-group.
We call a homomorphism of multiplicative
\Omega
-group

(2.3.15)

of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

or

-representation

if the map

f(a_{1}*b_{1})\bullet a_{2}=(f(a_{1})\bullet f(b_{1}))\bullet a_{2}

∎

Usually we identify a representation of the Abelian multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

and a left-side representation of the multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
.
However, if it is necessary for us,
we identify a representation of the Abelian multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

and a right-side representation of the multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
.

Definition 2.3.8
.

Left-side representation

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

is called

covariant

if the equality

a_{1}*(b_{1}*a_{2})=(a_{1}*b_{1})*a_{2}

is true.

∎

Definition 2.3.9
.

Left-side representation

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

is called

contravariant

if the equality

(2.3.17)

a_{1}^{-1}*(b_{1}^{-1}*a_{2})=(b_{1}*a_{1})^{-1}*a_{2}

is true.

∎

Theorem 2.3.10
.

The product

(a,b)\rightarrow a*b

in multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A

determines two different representations.

•

the
left shift

a^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}L(b)}\circ a=b*a

is left-side representation
of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A

in
\Omega
-algebra
A

(2.3.18)

L(c*b)=L(c)\circ L(b)

•

the
right shift

a^{\prime}=a\circ{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}R(b)}=a*b

is right-side representation
of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A

in
\Omega
-algebra
A

(2.3.19)

R(b*c)=R(b)\circ R(c)

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
10
]-
.
∎

Definition 2.3.11
.

Let
A_{1}
be
\Omega
-groupoid with product

(a,b)\rightarrow a*b

Let the map

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

be the left-side representation
of
\Omega
-groupoid
A_{1}

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
.
For any

a_{2}\in A_{2}
,

we define

orbit of representation

of the
\Omega
-groupoid
A_{1}
as set

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}A_{1}*a_{2}}=\{b_{2}=a_{1}*a_{2}:a_{1}\in A_{1}\}

∎

Definition 2.3.12
.

Let
A_{1}
be
\Omega
-groupoid with product

(a,b)\rightarrow a*b

Let the map

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{A_{2}}

of
\Omega

as set

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}a_{2}*A_{1}}=\{b_{2}=a_{2}*a_{1}:a_{1}\in A_{1}\}

∎

Theorem 2.3.13
.

Let the map

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

be the left-side representation
of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
.
Let

(2.3.20)

b_{2}\in A_{1}*a_{2}

Then

(2.3.21)

A_{1}*a_{2}=A_{1}*b_{2}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
10
]-
.
∎

Since left-side representation and right-side representation
are based on homomorphism of
\Omega
-group,
then the following statement is true.

Theorem 2.3.14

(duality principle for representation of multiplicative
\Omega
-group)
.

Any statement which holds for
left-side representation of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

holds also for right-side representation of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
, if
we will use right-side product over
A_{1}
-number
a_{1}
instead of
left-side product over
A_{1}
-number
a_{1}
.
∎

Theorem 2.3.15
.

If there exists single transitive representation

\textstyle{f:A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

of multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
,
then we can uniquely define coordinates on
A_{2}
using
A_{1}
-numbers.

If
f
is left-side single transitive representation
then
f(a)
is equivalent to the left shift
L(a)
on the group
A_{1}
.
If
f
is right-side single transitive representation
then
f(a)
is equivalent to the right shift
R(a)
on the group
A_{1}
.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
10
]-
.
∎

Theorem 2.3.16
.

Left and right shifts on multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
are commuting.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the associativity of product on multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}

(L(a)\circ c)\circ R(b)=(a*c)*b=a*(c*b)=L(a)\circ(c\circ R(b))

∎

Theorem 2.3.17
.

Let
A_{1}
be multiplicative
\Omega
-group.
Let left-side
A_{1}
-representation
f

on
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}
be single transitive.
Then we can uniquely define a single transitive right-side
A_{1}
-representation
h

on
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
A_{2}

such that diagram

\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})}
\textstyle{A_{2}}

is commutative for any
a_{1}
,
b_{1}\in A_{1}
.

Proof.
We use group coordinates for
A_{2}
-numbers
a_{2}
.
Then according to theorem
2.3.15

we can write the left shift
L(a_{1})
instead of the transformation
f(a_{1})
.

Let

a_{2}
,
b_{2}\in A_{2}
.

Then we can find
one and only one

a_{1}\in A_{1}

such that

b_{2}=a_{2}*a_{1}=a_{2}\circ R(a_{1})

We assume

h(a)=R(a)

For some

b_{1}\in A_{1}
,

we have

c_{2}=f(b_{1})\bullet a_{2}=L(b_{1})\circ a_{2}\ \ \ \ d_{2}=f(b_{1})\bullet b_{2}=L(b_{1})\circ b_{2}

According to the theorem
2.3.16
, the diagram

(2.3.22)

\textstyle{a_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})=R(a_{1})}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})=L(b_{1})}
\textstyle{b_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})=L(b_{1})}
\textstyle{c_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})=R(a_{1})}
\textstyle{d_{2}}

is commutative.

Changing
b_{1}
we get that
c_{2}
is an arbitrary
A_{2}
-number.

We see from the diagram that if
a_{2}=b_{2}
then
c_{2}=d_{2}
and therefore
h(e)=\delta
.
On other hand if
a_{2}\neq b_{2}
then
c_{2}\neq d_{2}
because
the left-side
A_{1}
-representation
f
is single transitive.
Therefore the right-side
A_{1}
-representation
h
is effective.

In the same way we can show that for given
c_{2}
we can find
a_{1}

such that

d_{2}=c_{2}\bullet h(a_{1})
.

Therefore the right-side
A_{1}
-representation
h
is single transitive.

In general the product of transformations of
the left-side
A_{1}
-representation
f
is not commutative and therefore
the right-side
A_{1}
-representation
h
is different from the left-side
A_{1}
-representation
f
.
In the same way we can create a left-side
A_{1}
-representation
f

using the right-side
A_{1}
-representation
h
.

∎

Representations
f
and
h
are called

twin representations

of the multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
.

Remark 2.3.18
.

It is clear that transformations
L(a)
and
R(a)

are different until the multiplicative
\Omega
-group
A_{1}
is nonabelian.
However they both are maps onto.
Theorem
2.3.17
states that if both
right and left shift presentations exist on the set
A_{2}
,
then we can define two commuting representations on the set
A_{2}
.
The right shift or the left shift only cannot represent both types of representation.
To understand why it is so let us change diagram
(
2.3.22
)
and assume

h(a_{1})\bullet a_{2}=L(a_{1})\circ a_{2}=b_{2}

instead of

a_{2}\bullet h(a_{1})=a_{2}\circ R(a_{1})=b_{2}

and let us see what expression
h(a_{1})
has at
the point
c_{2}
. The diagram

\textstyle{a_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})=L(a_{1})}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})=L(b_{1})}
\textstyle{b_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})=L(b_{1})}
\textstyle{c_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})}
\textstyle{d_{2}}

is equivalent to the diagram

\textstyle{a_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})=L(a_{1})}
\textstyle{b_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(b_{1})=L(b_{1})}
\textstyle{c_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h(a_{1})}
\scriptstyle{(f(b_{1}))^{-1}=L(b^{-1}_{1})}
\textstyle{d_{2}}

and we have

d_{2}=b_{1}b_{2}=b_{1}a_{1}a_{2}=b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}^{-1}c_{2}
.

Therefore

h(a_{1})\bullet c_{2}=(b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}^{-1})c_{2}

We see that the representation of
h

depends on its argument.

∎

2.4.
Tensor Product of Representations

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let

A
,
B_{1}
, …,
B_{n}
,
B

be universal algebras.
Let, for any

k
,
k=1
, …,
n
,

\textstyle{f_{k}:A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{B_{k}}

be effective representation of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
B_{k}
.
Let

\textstyle{f:A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{B}

be effective representation of
\Omega_{1}
-algebra
A

in
\Omega_{2}
-algebra
B
.
The map

r_{2}:B_{1}\times...\times B_{n}\rightarrow B

is called

reduced polymorphism of representations

f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}
into representation
f
,
if, for any

k
,
k=1
, …,
n
,

provided that all variables except the variable
x_{k}\in B_{k}

have given value, the map
r_{2}

is a reduced morphism of representation
f_{k}
into representation
f
.

If
f_{1}=...=f_{n}
, then we say that the map
r_{2}

is reduced polymorphism of representation
f_{1}
into representation
f
.

If
f_{1}=...=f_{n}=f
, then we say that the map
r_{2}

is reduced polymorphism of representation
f
.

∎

Definition 2.5.8
.

Theorem 2.5.9
.

If the set

.

Proof.

]-
.
∎

Remark 2.5.10
.

The proof of the theorem

2.5.9

gives us effective method for constructing the quasibasis of the representation
f
.
Choosing an arbitrary generating set, step by step, we
remove from set those elements which have coordinates
relative to other elements of the set.
If the generating set of the representation is infinite,
then this construction may not have the last step.
If the representation has finite generating set,
then we need a finite number of steps to construct a quasibasis of this representation.

∎

We introduced
\Omega_{2}
-word of
x\in A_{2}
relative generating set
X

in the definition

2.5.6
.
From the theorem

2.5.9
,
it follows that if the generating set
X
is not an quasibasis,
then a choice of
\Omega_{2}
-word relative generating set
X
is ambiguous.
However, even if the generating set
X
is an quasibasis,
then a representation of
m\in A_{2}
in form of
\Omega_{2}
-word is ambiguous.

Remark 2.5.11
.

There are three reasons of ambiguity in notation of

\Omega_{2}
-word.

2.5.11.1:

In
\Omega_{i}
-algebra
A_{i}
,
i=1
,
2
,
equalities may be defined.
For instance, if

then the equality

is true for any

.

2.5.11.2:

\Omega_{2}
-word
may be associated with properties of representation.
For instance, if
m_{1}
, …,

are

-words,

\omega\in\Omega_{2}(n)
and

,

then

2.10

2.10

For instance, let

.
The equation (

)
has the form of distributive law

a(b^{1}e_{1}+b^{2}e_{2})=(ab^{1})e_{1}+(ab^{2})e_{2}

(2.5.2)

f(a)(m_{1}...m_{n}\omega)=(f(a)(m_{1}))...(f(a)(m_{n}))\omega

At the same time, if

is operation of

\Omega_{1}
-algebra

(2.5.3)

2.5.11.3:

Therefore we can ignore this

[

]-

So we see that we can define different equivalence relations

Theorem 2.5.13
.

2.6.
Diagram of Representations of Universal Algebras

Definition 2.6.1
.

Diagram
(f,A)
of representations of universal algebras

is oriented graph such that

2.6.1.1:

the vertex of
A_{k}
,
k=1
, …,
n
, is
\Omega_{k}
-algebra;

2.6.1.2:

the edge
f_{kl}
is representation
of
\Omega_{k}
-algebra
A_{k}
in

\Omega_{l}
-algebra
A_{l}
;

We require that this graph
is connected graph and does not have loops.
Let
A_{[0]}
be set of initial vertices of the graph.
Let
A_{[k]}
be set of vertices of the graph
for which the maximum path from the initial vertices is
k
.

∎

Remark 2.6.2
.

Since different vertices of the graph
can be the same algebra,
then we denote

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})

the set of universal algebras which are distinct.
From the equality

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})=(A_{1}\ \ ...\ \ A_{n})

it follows that, for any index
(i)
,
there exists at least one index
i
such that

A_{(i)}=A_{i}
.

If there are two sets of sets

A=(A_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ A_{(n)})
,

B=(B_{(1)}\ \ ...\ \ B_{(n)})
a
nd there is a map

h_{(i)}:A_{(i)}\rightarrow B_{(i)}

for an index
(i)
,
then also there is a map

h_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}

for any index
i
such that

A_{(i)}=A_{i}

and in this case
h_{i}=h_{(i)}
.

∎

2.7.
Permutation

Definition 2.7.1
.

An injective map of finite set into itself is called

permutation
.
2.17
2.17

2.17

You can see definition and properties of permutation in
[
2
], pages 27 - 32,
[
14
], pages 58, 59.

∎
∎

Usually we write a permutation
\sigma
as a matrix

(2.7.1)

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&...&a_{n}\\
\sigma(a_{1})&...&\sigma(a_{n})\end{pmatrix}

The notation
(
2.7.1
)
is equivalent to the statement

\sigma:a\in A\rightarrow\sigma(a)\in A\ \ \ A=\{a_{1},...,a_{n}\}

So the order of columns in the notation
(
2.7.1
)
is not essential.

Since there is an order
on the set

\{a_{1},...,a_{n}\}

(for instance, we assume, that
a_{i}
precedes
a_{j}

when
i<j
),
then we may assume that elements of first row
are written according to the intended order
and we will identify permutation with second row

(2.7.2)

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(a_{1})&...&\sigma(a_{n})\end{pmatrix}

Definition 2.7.2
.

The map

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}|\sigma|}:\sigma\in S(n)\rightarrow\{-1,1\}

defined by the equality

(2.7.3)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}|\sigma|}=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&\textrm{permutation }\sigma\textrm{ even}\\
-1&\textrm{permutation }\sigma\textrm{ odd}\end{matrix}\right.

is called

parity of permutation
.

∎

3.2.
D
-Module Type

Organization of coordinates of vector in matrix
is called
D
-module type.

In this section, we consider column vector
and row vector.
it is evident that there exist other forms of representation of vector.
For instance, we can represent coordinates of vector as

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}\times\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}
matrix or as triangular matrix.
Format of representation depends on considered problem.

Definition 3.2.1
.

We represented the set of vectors

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=v_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,

as
row of matrix

and the set of
D_{(1)}
-nummbers

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=c^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}})=c^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as

(3.2.1)

...\\
c^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

-module

is called

and

Theorem 3.2.2
.

If we write vectors of basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

as row of matrix

(3.2.2)

e=\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

and coordinates of vector

\overline{w}{}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

with respect to basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
as
column of matrix

(3.2.3)

w=\begin{pmatrix}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

then we can represent the vector

\overline{w}{}

as product of matrices

(3.2.4)

\overline{w}{}=we=\begin{pmatrix}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
11
]-
.
∎

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Coordinates of vector
v\in V
relative to basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

of free
D
-module
V

are uniquely defined.
The equality

(3.2.5)

ve=we\ \ \ \ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

implies the equality

v=w\ \ \ \ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
11
]-
.
∎

Definition 3.2.4
.

We represented the set of vectors

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
column of matrix

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

and the set of
D_{(1)}
-nummbers

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}})=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
row of matrix

(3.2.6)

c=\begin{pmatrix}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

Corresponding representation of
D
-module
V
is called

D
-module of rows
,
and
V
-number is called

row vector
.

∎

.

If a statement depends on the format of representation of vector,
we will specify either type of
D
-module,
or format of representation of basis.
Both ways of specifying the type of
D
-module are equivalent.

3.3.

D
-Module

3.3.1.
General Definition

Definition 3.3.1
.

Morphism of representations

(3.3.1)

(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

of
D_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into
D_{2}
-module
V_{2}

is called homomorphism or

of
D_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into
D_{2}
-module
V_{2}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\mathcal{L}(D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2};V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})}

of

-module
V_{1}

into
D_{2}
-module
V_{2}
.

∎

3.4

In some books

f\circ(a+b)=f\circ a+f\circ b

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\End(D,V)}

set of endomorphisms
of
D
-module
V
.

∎

Theorem 3.3.14
.

The set

\End(D,V)

of endomorphisms of
D
-module
V

is
D
-module.

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
11
]-
.
∎

3.4.
Algebra over Commutative Ring

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
D
be commutative ring.

D
-module
A
is called

algebra over ring

D

or

D
-algebra
,
if we defined product
3.10
3.10

3.10

I follow the definition
given in
[
17
], page 1,
[
12
], page 4. The statement which
is true for any
D
-module,
is true also for
D
-algebra.

in
A

(3.4.1)

v\,w=C\circ(v,w)

where
C
is bilinear map

C:A\times A\rightarrow A

If
A
is free

D
-module, then
A
is called

free algebra over ring

D
.

∎

Theorem 3.4.2
.

The multiplication in the algebra
A
is distributive over addition

(3.4.2)

(a+b)c=ac+bc

(3.4.3)

a(b+c)=ab+ac

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem [
11
]-
.
∎

Definition 3.4.3
.

If the product in
D
-algebra
A
has unit element,
then
D
-algebra
A
is called

unital algebra

3.11
3.11

3.11

See the definition of unital
D
-algebra also on the pages
[
3
]-137.

∎

The multiplication in algebra can be neither commutative
nor associative. Following definitions are based
on definitions given in [
17
], page 13.

Definition 3.4.4
.

The
commutator

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}[a,b]}=ab-ba

measures commutativity in
D
-algebra
A
.

D
-algebra
A
is called

commutative
,
if

[a,b]=0

∎

Definition 3.4.5
.

The
associator

(3.4.4)

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}(a,b,c)}=(ab)c-a(bc)

measures associativity in
D
-algebra
A
.

D
-algebra
A
is called

associative
,
if

(a,b,c)=0

∎

Definition 3.4.6
.

The set
3.12
3.12

3.12

The definition is based on
the similar definition in
[
17
], p. 13.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}N(A)}=\{a\in A:\forall b,c\in A,(a,b,c)=(b,a,c)=(b,c,a)=0\}

is called the

nucleus of an
D
-algebra
A
.

∎

Definition 3.4.7
.

The set
3.13
3.13

3.13

The definition is based on
the similar definition in
[
17
], page 14.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}Z(A)}=\{a\in A:a\in N(A),\forall b\in A,ab=ba\}

is called the

center of an
D
-algebra
A_{1}
.

∎

Convention 3.4.8
.

According to definitions

3.1.16
,

3.4.1
,
free
D
-algebra
A
is

D
-module
A
,
which has a basis

\overline{\overline{e}}
.
However, generally speaking,

\overline{\overline{e}}
is not a
basis of
D
-algebra
A
,
because there is product in
D
-algebra
A
.
For instance, in quaternion algebra, any quaternion

a=a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 0}}}+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}i+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}j+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}k

can be represented as

a=a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 0}}}+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}i+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 2}}}j+a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 3}}}ij

However, for most problems
using a basis of
D
-module
A
is easier than
using a basis of
D
-algebra
A
.
For instance, it is easier to determine coordinates of
H
-number with respect to the basis

(1,i,j,k)

of
R
-vector space
H
,
than to determine coordinates of
H
-number with respect to the basis

(1,i,j)

of
R
-algebra
H
.
Therefore the phrase ”we consider basis of
D
-algebra
A
”
means that we consider
D
-algebra
A

and basis of
D
-module
A
.

∎

Convention 3.4.9
.

Let
A
be free algebra
with finite or countable basis.
Considering expansion of element of algebra
A
relative basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

we use the same root letter to denote this element and its coordinates.
In expression
a^{2}
, it is not clear whether this is component
of expansion of element

a
relative basis, or this is operation
a^{2}=aa
.
To make text clearer we use separate color for index of element
of algebra. For instance,

a=a^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

∎

D
-algebra
A
inherits type of
D
-module
A
.

Definition 3.4.10
.

D
-algebra
A
is called

D
-algebra of columns,
if
D
-module
A

is
D
-module of columns.

∎

.

Let

\overline{\overline{e}}
be the basis of
D
-algebra of columns
A
.
If
D
-algebra
A
has unit,
then we assume that
e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 0}}}

D
-algebra
A
.

∎

.

D
-algebra

is called

D

is
D

∎

.

Let

\overline{\overline{e}}
be the basis of
D

.

D

.

∎

3.5.
Linear Map of
D
-Algebra

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
A_{1}
and

be algebras over commutative ring
D
.
The linear map
of the
D
-module
A_{1}

into the
D
-module
A_{2}

is called

of
D
-algebra
A_{1}
into
D
-algebra
A_{2}
.

Let us denote

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\mathcal{L}(D;A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2})}

of
D
-algebra

A_{1}

into
D
-algebra

A_{2}
.

∎

Definition 3.5.2
.

Let
A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}
,

be
D
-algebras.

of

, …,

into
D
-module

is called

of

-algebras

A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}

into
D
-algebra
S
.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\mathcal{L}(D;A_{1}\times...\times A_{n}\rightarrow S)}

of
D
-algebras

A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}

into
D
-algebra

.

-linear maps
of
D
-algebra
A_{1}
(
A_{1}=...=A_{n}=A_{1}
)
into
D
-algebra

S
.
∎
∎

Theorem 3.5.4
.

Definition 4.1.10
.

Let
A
be left-side module.
Let

v=(v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})\in V,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

be set of vectors.
The expression

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.9}\sum c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})}
,
c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})\in A_{(1)}
,

is called

linear combination
of vectors

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
.

A vector

w=\sum c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})

is called

linearly dependent

on vectors

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
.

∎

Convention 4.1.11
.

We will use summation convention
in which repeated index
in linear combination
implies summation with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}}c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})

If needed to clearly show a set of indices, I will do it.

∎

Theorem 4.1.18
.

Let
A
be associative
D
-algebra.
Let
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
be quasibasis of left
A
-module
V
.
Let

(4.1.36)

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

be linear dependence of vectors of the quasibasis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
.
Then

4.1.18.1:

A_{(1)}
-number

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

does not have inverse element
in
D
-algebra
A_{(1)}
.

4.1.18.2:

The set
A^{\prime}
of matrices

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

generates left
A
-module
A^{\prime}
.

Proof.
Let there exist matrix

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

such that the equality
(
4.1.36
)
is true and there exist index

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}

such that

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}\neq 0
.

If we assume that
A
-number

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

has inverse one, then the equality

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}\}}(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){})^{-1}c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}){}e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}){}

follows from the equality
(
4.1.36
).
Therefore, the vector

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

is linear combination of other vectors of the set
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

and the set
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
is not quasibasis.
Therefore, our assumption is false,
and
A_{(1)}
-number

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

does not have inverse.

Let matrices

b=(b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in A^{\prime}
,

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in A^{\prime}
.

From equalities

b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

it follows that

(b(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})+c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}))e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

Therefore, the set
A^{\prime}
is Abelian group.

Let matrix

c=(c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}),\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})\in A^{\prime}

and
a\in A
.
From the equality

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

it follows that

(ac(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}))e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

Therefore, Abelian group
A^{\prime}
is left
A
-module.

∎

Theorem 4.1.22
.

The left
A
-vector space
is free
A
-module.

Proof.
Let the set of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

be linear dependent. Then the equation

w(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=0

implies existence of index
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}
such that

w(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}){}\neq 0
.

According to the theorem

4.1.12
,
the vector

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}})

linearly depends on rest of vectors of the set
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
.
According to the definition

4.1.15
,
the set of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

is not a basis for left
A
-vector space
V
.

Therefore, if the set of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

is a basis, then these vectors
are linearly independent.
Since an arbitrary vector
v\in V

is linear combination of vectors

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

then the set of vectors
v
,

e(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})
,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

is not linearly independent.

∎

.

We can represent linear combination

\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

of vectors

v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product of matrices

(4.2.16)

c{}_{*}{}^{*}v=\begin{pmatrix}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}{}_{*}{}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from definitions

4.1.10
,

4.2.8
.

∎

Theorem 4.2.10
.

If we write vectors of basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

as column of matrix

(4.2.17)

e=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

and coordinates of vector

\overline{w}{}=w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

with respect to basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}
as
row of matrix

(4.2.18)

w=\begin{pmatrix}w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}

then we can represent the vector

\overline{w}{}

as
{}_{*}{}^{*}
-product of matrices

(4.2.19)

\overline{w}{}=w{}_{*}{}^{*}e=\begin{pmatrix}w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}{}_{*}{}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it n}}}\end{pmatrix}=w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem

4.2.9
.

∎

Theorem 4.2.11
.

Coordinates of vector
v\in V
relative to basis
\overline{\overline{e}}{}

of left free
A
-module
V

are uniquely defined.
The equality

(4.2.20)

v{}_{*}{}^{*}e=w{}_{*}{}^{*}e\ \ \ \ v_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

implies the equality

v=w\ \ \ \ v_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem

4.1.23
.

∎

Theorem 4.2.12
.

Let the set of vectors

\overline{\overline{e}}{}=(e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}},\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it K}})

be a basis of
D
-algebra of rows
A
.
Let

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{1}{}=(e_{1}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}},\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

be a basis of left
A
-module of rows
V
.

Then the set of
V
-numbers

(4.2.21)

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{2}{}=(e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}=e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}})

is the basis of
D
-module
V
.
The basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{2}
is called extension of the basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{1}
.

Lemma 4.2.13
.

The set of
V
-numbers
e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}

generates
D
-module
V
.

Proof.

Let

(4.2.22)

a=a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}e_{1}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

be any
V
-number.
For any
A
-number
a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}
,
according to the definition

3.4.1

and the convention

3.4.8
,
there exist
D
-numbers
a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}
such that

(4.2.23)

a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}

The equality

(4.2.24)

a=a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}e^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it k}}}e_{1}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}

follows from equalities
(

),
(
4.2.22
),
(
4.2.23
).
According to the theorem

3.1.7
,
the lemma
follows from the equality
(
4.2.24
).

\odot

Lemma 4.2.14
.

The set of
V
-numbers
e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}

is linearly independent over the ring
D
.

Proof.

Let

(4.2.25)

a_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}e_{2}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it ik}}}=0

The equality

(4.2.26)

(

),
(
4.2.25
).
According to the theorem

4.2.11
,
the equality

(4.2.27)

(

).

,
the equality

).
Therefore,

.

\odot

Proof of theorem

.

-module
V

of left

-module

.

∎

Theorem 4.3.2
.

Let

v=(v_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}\in V,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

-module
V
.

v_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

of left

-module

,
then linear combination of vectors

,
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}
,

belongs submodule
V^{\prime}
.

Proof.
The theorem follows from
the theorem

4.1.7

and definitions

4.1.10
,

4.3.1
.

A_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into left
A_{2}
-module
V_{2}
.

∎

We will use notation

f\circ a=f(a)

for image of homomorphism
f
.

Theorem 5.1.2
.

The homomorphism

(
5.1.3
)
(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

of left
A_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into left
A_{2}
-module
V_{2}

satisfies following equalities

(5.1.4)

h(p+q)=h(p)+h(q)

(5.1.5)

h(pq)=h(p)h(q)

(5.1.6)

g\circ(a+b)=g\circ a+g\circ b

(5.1.7)

g\circ(ab)=(g\circ a)(g\circ b)

(5.1.8)

g\circ(pa)=h(p)(g\circ a)

(5.1.9)

f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

(5.1.10)

f\circ(av)=(g\circ a)(f\circ v)

p,q\in D_{1}\ \ \ \,a,b\in A_{1}\ \ \ \,u,v\in V_{1}

Proof.

According to definitions

2.1.4
,

2.6.5
,

4.1.1
,

the map

(5.1.11)

(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2})

is homomorphism
of
D_{1}
-algebra
A_{1}

into
D_{1}
-algebra
A_{2}
.
Therefore, equalities

(
5.1.4
),
(
5.1.5
),

(
5.1.6
),
(
5.1.7
),
(
5.1.8
)

follow from the theorem

3.3.2
.

The equality
(
5.1.9
)
follows from the definition

5.1.1
,
since, accorfing to the definition

2.1.4
,
the map
f
is homomorpism of Abelian group.
The equality
(
5.1.10
)
follows from the equality
(
2.1.6
)
because the map

(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

is morphism of representation
g_{1.34}

into representation
g_{2.34}
.

∎

Definition 5.1.3
.

Homomorphism
5.1

(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

-module

which is homomorphism.

Theorem 5.2.2
.

The homomorphism

(
5.2.3
)
(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

of left
A_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into left
A_{2}
-module
V_{2}

satisfies following equalities

(5.2.4)

g\circ(a+b)=g\circ a+g\circ b

(5.2.5)

g\circ(ab)=(g\circ a)(g\circ b)

(5.2.6)

g\circ(pa)=p(g\circ a)

(5.2.7)

f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

(5.2.8)

f\circ(av)=(g\circ a)(f\circ v)

p\in D\ \ \ \,a,b\in A_{1}\ \ \ \,u,v\in V_{1}

Proof.

According to definitions

2.1.4
,

2.6.5
,

4.1.1
,

the map

(5.2.9)

g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}

is homomorphism
of
D
-algebra
A_{1}

into
D_{1}
-algebra
A
.
Therefore, equalities

(
5.2.4
),
(
5.2.5
),
(
5.2.6
)

follow from the theorem

3.3.8
.

The equality
(
5.2.7
)
follows from the definition

5.2.1
,
since, accorfing to the definition

2.1.4
,
the map
f
is homomorpism of Abelian group.
The equality
(
5.2.8
)
follows from the equality
(
2.1.6
)
because the map

(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

is morphism of representation
g_{1.34}

into representation
g_{2.34}
.

∎

\overline{g}{}

relative to bases

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V}
,

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{W}
.
The equality

Therefore,
\overline{f}{}=\overline{g}{}

∎

Theorem 5.3.9
.

5.4.
Sum of Homomorphisms of
A
-Module

Theorem 5.4.1
.

Let
V
,
W
be left
A
-vector spaces
and maps

g:V\rightarrow W,

h:V\rightarrow W

be homomorphisms
of left
A
-vector space.
Let the map

f:V\rightarrow W

be defined by the equality

(5.4.1)

\forall v\in V:f\circ v=g\circ v+h\circ v

The map
f
is homomorphism
of left
A
-vector space
and is called

sum

f=g+h

of homomorphisms

g
and
h
.

Proof.
According to the theorem

4.1.5

(the equality
(
4.1.13
)),
the equality

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle f\circ(u+v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ(u+v)+h\circ(u+v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ u+g\circ v

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle h\circ u+h\circ u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\circ u+f\circ v

follows from the equality

(
5.3.4
)
f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

and the equality
(
5.4.1
).
According to the theorem

4.1.5

(the equality
(
4.1.15
)),
the equality

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle f\circ(av)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ(av)+h\circ(av)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(g\circ v)+a(h\circ v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(g\circ v+h\circ v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a(f\circ v)

follows from the equality

(
5.3.5
)
f\circ(av)=a(f\circ v)

and the equality
(
5.4.1
).

The theorem follows from the theorem

5.3.2

and equalities
(
5.4.2
),
(
5.4.3
).

∎

,

Theorem 5.5.1
.

,

,

A
-vector spaces.

(5.5.1)

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{W}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}

(5.5.2)

\displaystyle\forall u\in U:f\circ u

)

and the equality
(
5.4.1
).
The theorem follows from the theorem

),
(

).

6.1.
Right Module over Associative

-Algebra

Let

be commutative associative ring with unit.

Definition 6.1.1
.

-algebra.
Let

Let in

Let there exist homomorphism

g_{34}:A\rightarrow\End(V)

of

-algebra

into

-algebra

\End(D,V)
.

Effective right-side representation

(6.1.1)

\begin{matrix}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 16.32811pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.32811pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{g_{34}:A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.62932pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{*}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.32811pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.32811pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces&g_{34}(a):v\in V\rightarrow va\in V&a\in A\end{matrix}

of

-algebra
A

in
D
-module

is called

over
D
-algebra

Definition 6.1.10
.

Let
A
be right-side module.
Let

v=(v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})\in V,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}})

Convention 6.1.11
.

We will use summation convention
in which repeated index
in linear combination
implies summation with respect to repeated index.
In this case we assume that we know the set
of summation index and do not use summation symbol

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}}}v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})

If needed to clearly show a set of indices, I will do it.

e{}_{*}{}^{*}v=e{}_{*}{}^{*}w\ \ \ \ e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=e_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

implies the equality

v=w\ \ \ \ v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=w^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from the theorem

6.1.23
.

∎

.

6.2.2.
Right
A
-Module of Rows

Definition 6.2.8
.

We represented the set of vectors

v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
column of matrix

v=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

and the set of
A_{(1)}
-nummbers

c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}})=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}})=c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
row of matrix

(6.2.15)

c=\begin{pmatrix}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}

Corresponding representation of right
A
-module
V
is called

right
A
-module of rows
,
and
V
-number is called

row vector
.

∎

Theorem 6.2.9
.

We can represent linear combination

\sum_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}=\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}v(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})c(\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}})=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

of vectors

v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}
, …,
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}

as
{}^{*}{}_{*}
-product of matrices

(6.2.16)

v{}^{*}{}_{*}c=\begin{pmatrix}v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}\\
...\\
v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}{}^{*}{}_{*}\begin{pmatrix}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it 1}}}&...&c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it m}}}\end{pmatrix}=v^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}c_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

Proof.
The theorem follows from definitions

6.1.10
,

6.2.8
.

∎

6.2.9
.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

The homomorphism

(
7.1.3
)
(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

of right
A_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into right
A_{2}
-module
V_{2}

satisfies following equalities

(7.1.4)

h(p+q)=h(p)+h(q)

(7.1.5)

h(pq)=h(p)h(q)

(7.1.6)

g\circ(a+b)=g\circ a+g\circ b

(7.1.7)

g\circ(ab)=(g\circ a)(g\circ b)

(7.1.8)

g\circ(ap)=(g\circ a)h(p)

(7.1.9)

f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

(7.1.10)

f\circ(va)=(f\circ v)(g\circ a)

p,q\in D_{1}\ \ \ \,a,b\in A_{1}\ \ \ \,u,v\in V_{1}

Proof.

According to definitions

2.1.4
,

2.6.5
,

6.1.1
,

the map

(7.1.11)

(h:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{2}\ \ \,g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2})

is homomorphism
of
D_{1}
-algebra
A_{1}

into
D_{1}
-algebra
A_{2}
.
Therefore, equalities

(
7.1.4
),
(
7.1.5
),

(
7.1.6
),
(
7.1.7
),
(
7.1.8
)

follow from the theorem

3.3.2
.

The equality
(
7.1.9
)
follows from the definition

7.1.1
,
since, accorfing to the definition

2.1.4
,
the map
f
is homomorpism of Abelian group.
The equality
(
7.1.10
)
follows from the equality
(
2.1.6
)
because the map

(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

is morphism of representation
g_{1.34}

into representation
g_{2.34}
.

∎

\displaystyle e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}(g\circ v)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}(g\circ w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)+\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}w)

follows from equalities

(

),
it follows that the map
\overline{f}{}
is homomorphism
of Abelian group.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}v))(\overline{g}{}\circ a)

follows from equalities

)

)

(

Theorem 7.2.2
.

The homomorphism

(
7.2.3
)
(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

of right
A_{1}
-module
V_{1}

into right
A_{2}
-module
V_{2}

satisfies following equalities

(7.2.4)

g\circ(a+b)=g\circ a+g\circ b

(7.2.5)

g\circ(ab)=(g\circ a)(g\circ b)

(7.2.6)

g\circ(ap)=(g\circ a)p

(7.2.7)

f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

(7.2.8)

f\circ(va)=(f\circ v)(g\circ a)

p\in D\ \ \ \,a,b\in A_{1}\ \ \ \,u,v\in V_{1}

Proof.

According to definitions

2.1.4
,

2.6.5
,

6.1.1
,

the map

(7.2.9)

g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}

is homomorphism
of
D
-algebra
A_{1}

into
D_{1}
-algebra
A
.
Therefore, equalities

(
7.2.4
),
(
7.2.5
),
(
7.2.6
)

follow from the theorem

3.3.8
.

The equality
(
7.2.7
)
follows from the definition

7.2.1
,
since, accorfing to the definition

2.1.4
,
the map
f
is homomorpism of Abelian group.
The equality
(
7.2.8
)
follows from the equality
(
2.1.6
)
because the map

(g:A_{1}\rightarrow A_{2}\ \ \,f:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})

is morphism of representation
g_{1.34}

into representation
g_{2.34}
.

∎

D
-algebra
A

into
D
-algebra
A_{(1)}
.

The equality

(7.2.58)

v(d+0)=vd+v0=vd+0=vd

follows from the equality

(

)
v(p+q)=vp+vq

The theorem follows from the equality
(
7.2.58
)
and the definition

2.1.4
.

∎

∎

\overline{f}{}\circ e_{V_{1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}=e_{V_{2}}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}{f}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}}

relative to basis

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V_{2}}
.
The equality

(7.3.24)

\overline{w}{}=e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}v

follows from equalities
(
7.3.22
),
(
7.3.23
).
The equality
(
7.3.7
)
follows from comparison of
(

)
and (
7.3.24
) and
the theorem

6.2.4
.
From the equality
(
7.3.23
)
and from the theorem

6.2.4
,
it follows that the matrix
f
is unique.

∎

Theorem 7.3.8
.

Let

f=(f_{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}}^{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}},\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it i}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it I}},\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it j}}\in\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.6}\it J}})

be matrix of

-numbers.

(
7.3.6
)

-module of columns

)

is unique.

Proof.

(7.3.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}(v+w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}v+e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)+\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}w)

follows from equalities

(
2.2.11
)
a{}_{*}{}^{*}(b_{1}+b_{2})=a{}_{*}{}^{*}b_{1}+a{}_{*}{}^{*}b_{2}

(
7.2.8
)
f\circ(va)=(f\circ v)a

(
7.2.16
)
\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)=e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}(\overline{g}{}\circ v)

From the equality
(
7.3.30
),
it follows that the map
\overline{f}{}
is homomorphism
of Abelian group.
The equality

(7.3.31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}(va)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(e_{V_{2}}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}v)a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{V_{1}}{}_{*}{}^{*}v))a

follows from equalities

(

)

),

2.6.5
,

,

of

-module of columns
V_{1}

into

-module of columns

.

7.4.
Sum of Homomorphisms of
A
-Module

Theorem 7.4.1
.

Let
V
,
W
be right
A
-vector spaces
and maps

g:V\rightarrow W,

h:V\rightarrow W

be homomorphisms
of right
A
-vector space.
Let the map

f:V\rightarrow W

be defined by the equality

(7.4.1)

\forall v\in V:f\circ v=g\circ v+h\circ v

The map
f
is homomorphism
of right
A
-vector space
and is called

sum

f=g+h

of homomorphisms

g
and
h
.

Proof.
According to the theorem

6.1.5

(the equality
(
6.1.13
)),
the equality

(7.4.2)

\displaystyle f\circ(u+v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ(u+v)+h\circ(u+v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ u+g\circ v

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle h\circ u+h\circ u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\circ u+f\circ v

follows from the equality

(
7.3.4
)
f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

and the equality
(
7.4.1
).
According to the theorem

6.1.5

(the equality
(
6.1.15
)),
the equality

(7.4.3)

\displaystyle f\circ(va)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\circ(va)+h\circ(va)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\circ v)a+(h\circ v)a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\circ v+h\circ v)a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f\circ v)a

follows from the equality

(
7.3.5
)
f\circ(va)=(f\circ v)a

and the equality
(
7.4.1
).

The theorem follows from the theorem

7.3.2

and equalities
(
7.4.2
),
(
7.4.3
).

∎

Theorem 7.4.2
.

Let
V
,
W
be
right
A
-vector spaces of columns.
Let

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V}
be a basis of right
A
-vector space
V
.
Let

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{W}
be a basis of right
A
-vector space
W
.
The homomorphism

\overline{f}{}:V\rightarrow W

is sum of homomorphisms

\overline{g}{}:V\rightarrow W
,

\overline{h}{}:V\rightarrow W

iff
matrix
f
of homomorphism
\overline{f}{}

relative to bases

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V}
,

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{W}

is equal to the sum of the matrix
g
of the homomorphism
\overline{g}{}

relative to bases

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V}
,

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{W}

and the matrix
h
of the homomorphism
\overline{h}{}

relative to bases

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{V}
,

\overline{\overline{e}}{}_{W}
.

Proof.
According to theorem

7.3.6

(7.4.4)

\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}a)=e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}a

(7.4.5)

\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}a)=e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}a

(7.4.6)

\overline{h}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}a)=e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}a

According to the theorem

7.4.1
,
the equality

(7.4.7)

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)+\overline{h}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}v+e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}(g+h){}_{*}{}^{*}v

follows from equalities
(
2.2.11
),
(
2.2.12
),
(
7.4.5
),
(
7.4.6
).
The equality

f=g+h

follows from equalities
(
7.4.4
),
(
7.4.7
)
and the theorem

7.3.6
.

Let

f=g+h

According to the theorem

7.3.8
,
there exist homomorphisms

\overline{f}{}:U\rightarrow V
,

\overline{g}{}:U\rightarrow V
,

\overline{h}{}:U\rightarrow V

such that

(7.4.8)

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}(g+h){}_{*}{}^{*}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}v+e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)+\overline{h}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)

From the equality
(
7.4.8
)
and the theorem

7.4.1
,
it follows that
the homomorphism
\overline{f}{}

is sum of homomorphisms
\overline{g}{}
,
\overline{h}{}
.

∎

.

,

be

According to the theorem

follows from equalities

7.5.
Product of Homomorphisms of
A
-Module

Theorem 7.5.1
.

Let
U
,
V
,
W
be right
A
-vector spaces.
Let diagram of maps

(7.5.1)

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{W}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}

(7.5.2)

\displaystyle\forall u\in U:f\circ u

\displaystyle=(h\circ g)\circ u

\displaystyle=h\circ(g\circ u)

be commutative diagram
where maps
g
,
h
are
homomorphisms of right
A
-vector space.
The map

f=h\circ g

is homomorphism
of right
A
-vector space
and is called

product of homomorphisms

h
,
g
.

Proof.
The equality
(
7.5.2
)
follows from the commutativity of the diagram
(
7.5.1
).
The equality

(7.5.3)

\displaystyle f\circ(u+v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\circ(g\circ(u+v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\circ(g\circ u+g\circ v))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h\circ(g\circ u)+h\circ(g\circ v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\circ u+f\circ v

follows from the equality

(
7.3.4
)
f\circ(u+v)=f\circ u+f\circ v

and the equality
(
7.5.2
).
The equality

(7.5.4)

\displaystyle f\circ(va)

\displaystyle=h\circ(g\circ(va))

\displaystyle=h\circ((g\circ v)a)

\displaystyle=(h\circ(g\circ v))a

\displaystyle=(f\circ v)a

follows from the equality

(
7.3.5
)
f\circ(va)=(f\circ v)a

and the equality
(
7.4.1
).
The theorem follows from the theorem

7.3.2

and equalities
(
7.5.3
),
(
7.5.4
).

∎

Theorem 7.5.3
.

(7.5.8)

\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u)=e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}u

(7.5.9)

\overline{h}{}\circ(e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}v)=e_{W}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}v

According to the theorem

7.5.1
,
the equality

(7.5.10)

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{h}{}\circ(\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{h}{}\circ(u{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}e_{V})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{W}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}u

follows from equalities
(
7.5.8
),
(
7.5.9
).
The equality
(
7.5.5
)
follows from equalities
(
7.5.7
),
(
7.5.10
)
and the theorem

7.3.6
.

Let

f=g{}_{*}{}^{*}h.

According to the theorem

7.3.8
,
there exist homomorphisms

\overline{f}{}:U\rightarrow V
,

\overline{g}{}:U\rightarrow V
,

\overline{h}{}:U\rightarrow V

such that

(7.5.11)

\displaystyle\overline{f}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u)=e_{W}{}_{*}{}^{*}f{}_{*}{}^{*}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{W}{}_{*}{}^{*}h{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{h}{}\circ(e_{V}{}_{*}{}^{*}g{}_{*}{}^{*}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{h}{}\circ(\overline{g}{}\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\overline{h}{}\circ\overline{g}{})\circ(e_{U}{}_{*}{}^{*}u)

From the equality
(
7.5.11
)
and the theorem

7.4.1
,
it follows that
the homomorphism
\overline{f}{}

is product of homomorphisms
\overline{h}{}
,
\overline{g}{}
.

∎

Proof.
